Description: Share, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, Secondary Actions: Expand\n\t\t\t\t86 container abstract\n\t\t\t\t\t87 heading Abstract, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t88 text Abstract\n\t\t\t\t\t89 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t90 text This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water distribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable real-time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study covers data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order linear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data with step-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately \n\t\t\t\t\t\t91 container plus or minus 1 normal m, ID: i001\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t92 container plus or minus 1 normal m\n\t\t\t\t\t\t93 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O, ID: i002\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t94 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O\n\t\t\t\t\t\t95 text  at the remote critical node, which is adequate for control-oriented modeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with a Smith predictor is designed using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evaluated in silico using real consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance under representative demand variability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within acceptable bounds while effectively rejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope of this study, the framework is explicitly conceived as a preparatory step for real-time deployment. The main conclusion is that a transparent, data-driven methodology grounded in field measurements can reduce modeling and tuning effort while yielding controller designs compatible with standard programmable logic controller (PLC) and supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms, enabling utilities to progress systematically toward practical real-time pressure control.\n\t\t\t\t96 container executive-summary\n\t\t\t\t\t97 heading Practical Applications, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t98 text Practical Applications\n\t\t\t\t\t99 text This study offers practical guidance for water utilities seeking to improve pressure management while reducing water losses and infrastructure wear. Using real operational data, the proposed approach shows how effective pressure regulation strategies can be derived directly from field measurements and implemented with standard PLC and SCADA systems already used by most utilities. As a result, the methodology is accessible without requiring advanced hydraulic-modeling expertise or specialized simulation tools. Pressure management is particularly important because daily demand variations often produce excessive pressures during low consumption periods (e.g., nighttime). These pressure surges increase the likelihood of pipe bursts, background leakage, and premature deterioration of network assets. The proposed control strategy addresses this issue by stabilizing pressure at a critical point in the network, allowing utilities to maintain pressures within acceptable and regulatory limits despite changing demand. While the controller has not yet been deployed in closed-loop field operation, the methodology is designed as a practical step toward real-time implementation. By reducing modeling effort and simplifying controller tuning, the approach lowers technical barriers for utilities interested in advanced pressure control and provides a realistic foundation for improving network reliability and reducing operational costs.\n\t\t\t\t100 container bodymatter\n\t\t\t\t\t101 container sec-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t102 heading Introduction, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t103 text Introduction\n\t\t\t\t\t\t104 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t105 text Water utilities must deliver potable water at adequate flow and pressure while minimizing water losses, operational costs, and asset deterioration. Modern water distribution networks (WDNs) exhibit pronounced intraday and seasonal demand variability, leading to large fluctuations in service pressure (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t106 link Bello et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c3-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t107 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t108 link Fan et al. 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c16-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t109 text ). When pressures exceed acceptable limits—particularly during low-demand periods—background leakage increases, pipe failure risk rises, and infrastructure lifetime is reduced (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t110 link Van Zyl et al. 2008, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c43-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t111 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t112 link Page 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c35-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t113 text ). Stabilizing and reducing pressure is therefore among the most effective strategies to mitigate leakage and bursts, with substantial long-term economic benefits (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t114 link Vicente et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c44-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t115 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t116 link Creaco and Walski 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c12-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t117 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t118 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t119 text In practice, pressure management typically relies on pressure-reducing valves (PRVs) operated under relatively simple control schemes (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t120 link Bello et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c3-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t121 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t122 link Campisano et al. 2010, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c5-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t123 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t124 link Creaco et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c10-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t125 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t126 link Galuppini et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c26-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t127 text ). A common solution is the mechanically piloted PRV with a fixed downstream setpoint (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t128 link Doghri et al. 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c15-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t129 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t130 link Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c17-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t131 text ). More advanced electronic add-on controllers modulate outlet pressure using predefined day–night profiles (time-based control) or as a function of inlet flow (flow-modulated control) to approximately maintain acceptable pressures at remote locations using surrogate signals (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t132 link Campisano et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c6-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t133 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t134 link Fontana et al. 2018b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t135 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t136 link Galuppini et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c26-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t137 text ). Remote real-time control extends these concepts by telemetering pressure at one or more critical nodes and continuously adjusting PRV setpoints; laboratory and field studies report effective leakage reduction and improved pressure stability (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t138 link Campisano et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c6-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t139 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t140 link Fontana et al. 2018a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c18-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t141 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t142 link b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t143 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t144 link Galuppini et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c26-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t145 text ). Comparative studies further confirm that remote-node–based regulation typically outperforms fixed, time-based, and flow-modulated strategies in pressure stability and leakage reduction, albeit with increased sensing and communication requirements (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t146 link Koşucu and Demirel 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c32-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t147 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t148 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t149 text Motivated by the limitations of fixed-pressure and open-loop modulation, research has increasingly focused on real-time control (RTC) strategies that continuously adjust PRV outlet pressure based on measured network conditions. Early numerical and laboratory investigations demonstrated the potential of RTC to reduce average pressure and leakage (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t150 link Campisano et al. 2010, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c5-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t151 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t152 link 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c7-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t153 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t154 link Creaco and Franchini 2013, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c11-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t155 text ). Subsequent studies introduced calibration methods for proportional or flow-dependent controllers (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t156 link Campisano et al. 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c7-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t157 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t158 link Page et al. 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c36-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t159 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t160 link Page and Creaco 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c34-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t161 text ) and explored RTC architectures for practical deployment (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t162 link Campisano et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c6-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t163 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t164 link Fontana et al. 2018a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c18-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t165 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t166 link b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t167 text ). Reviews identify RTC as a promising direction but emphasize the need for methodologies that integrate model development, controller design, and practical implementation guidance (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t168 link Vicente et al. 2016, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c44-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t169 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t170 link Creaco et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c10-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t171 text ). Recent work also highlights the importance of pressure regulation strategies that explicitly target leakage reduction under low-demand conditions, such as nighttime pressure management (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t172 link Denardi et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c14-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t173 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t174 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t175 text The dynamic behavior of PRVs has also been studied extensively. Prescott and Ulanicki (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t176 link 2003, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c37-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t177 text ) showed that PRVs exhibit strongly nonlinear dynamics, and that linear simplifications can miss important behaviors, particularly near low valve openings. More recent studies characterized PRV responses under varying operating conditions and control modes (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t178 link Doghri et al. 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c15-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t179 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t180 link Ferrarese and Malavasi 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c17-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t181 text ), while detailed analyses showed that electronically actuated PRVs may become unstable when valve–network gain increases sharply at small openings or when sampling is not properly selected (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t182 link Janus and Ulanicki 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c30-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t183 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t184 link 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c31-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t185 text ). These findings align with work emphasizing the need for controller-oriented dynamic models that explicitly account for delay and gain variation when PRVs act as feedback-control actuators (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t186 link Ulanicki and Beaujean 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c41-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t187 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t188 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t189 text At the same time, the availability of telemetry, remote actuators, and wireless communications has enabled a range of industrial pressure-control solutions. In many utilities, PRVs still operate in fixed-pressure mode (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t190 link Doghri et al. 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c15-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t191 text ). Commercial retrofit controllers offer scheduled, flow-based, adaptive, and closed-loop modes with telemetry and remote configuration (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t192 link Technolog Limited 2015, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c39-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t193 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t194 link 2017, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c40-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t195 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t196 link i2O Water Ltd. 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c28-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t197 text , \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t198 link 2013, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c29-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t199 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t200 link BERMAD Water Technologies 2023, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c4-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t201 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t202 link Cla-Val Company 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c9-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t203 text ), demonstrating the maturity of RTC technologies in operational settings.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t204 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t205 text Despite their effectiveness, commercial controllers are often configured through engineering judgment and heuristic tuning. Proprietary algorithms typically do not expose PRV–network dynamics, transport delays, or remote pressure interactions, limiting the ability to assess stability margins or predict closed-loop behavior under varying conditions (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t206 link Creaco et al. 2019, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c10-3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t207 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t208 link Janus and Ulanicki 2018, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c30-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t209 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t210 link Galuppini et al. 2020a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c20-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t211 text ). This motivates transparent, reproducible, model-based methodologies that support systematic tuning, predictable performance, and implementation on standard programmable logic controller (PLC)/supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t212 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t213 text More advanced schemes, including model predictive control, optimization-based approaches, and learning-based methods, have recently been proposed for multinode pressure regulation (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t214 link Galuppini et al. 2023a, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c24-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t215 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t216 link Castelletti et al. 2023, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c8-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t217 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t218 link Denardi et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c14-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t219 text ). Reinforcement-learning–based strategies combined with hydraulic simulation further illustrate the potential of data-driven control under uncertainty, albeit at the expense of increased modeling complexity, computational effort, and limited transparency (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t220 link Belfadil et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c2-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t221 text ). Similarly, recent work has explored integrated pressure management, energy recovery, and economic optimization frameworks (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t222 link Hammond et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c27-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t223 text ; \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t224 link Maio et al. 2024, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c33-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t225 text ). While promising, these approaches are often difficult to deploy within the constraints of typical district metered areas (DMAs), where pressure regulation is performed by a single PRV, and implementation simplicity, robustness, and interpretability remain critical.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t226 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t227 text Over the last few years, controller-oriented modeling and digital design/tuning workflows that explicitly leverage WDN measurements have been developed. In particular, Galuppini et al. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t228 link 2022, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c23-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t229 text ) proposed an in situ identification procedure that estimates frequency responses from measured input–output data via Blackman–Tukey spectral analysis and then fits parametric transfer-function models (including delay), specifically addressing the presence of colored exogenous disturbances. Complementarily, sum-of-delay gray-box structures have been introduced to represent the plant as a superposition of pure-delay contributions; due to the large number of candidate delays, sparse estimation (e.g., LASSO) can be used to reduce model complexity while preserving high-frequency features such as resonance peaks that are relevant for regulator design (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t230 link Galuppini et al. 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c22-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t231 text ). On the controller side, optimization-based tuning frameworks have been proposed to select digital regulator parameters under explicit robustness/performance constraints, including stability margin indicators and sampling/discretization-related limits (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t232 link Galuppini et al. 2020b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c21-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t233 text ).\n\t\t\t\t\t\t234 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t235 text However, despite these advances, few case studies report a complete and reproducible measurement-to-controller workflow on a real hydraulic sector using routine operational data and an implementation-ready control structure for standard industrial platforms. This work addresses this gap by proposing a complete, data-driven, experimentally grounded methodology for modeling and regulating pressure at a remote critical node in a real hydraulic sector. Although the controller is validated numerically rather than in closed-loop field operation, the methodology relies on measurements from the real network rather than on synthetic models. Specifically:\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t236 content list\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t237 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t238 text • A 20-day historical dataset of pressure and inflow is used to derive a stationary nonlinear relationship between flow and head losses between the PRV and the remote critical node.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t239 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t240 text • Step-response experiments on the existing PRV are used to identify a low-order linear dynamic model with a delay between the normalized pilot downstream setpoint, \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t241 container x Subscript p Baseline left parenthesis t right \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t244 container h Subscript m Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis, ID: i004\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t245 container h Subscript m Baseline left parenthesis t right parenthesis\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t246 text .\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t247 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t248 text • The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption, reproducing critical node pressure within \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t249 container plus or minus 1 normal m, ID: i005\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t250 container plus or minus 1 normal m\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t251 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O, ID: i006\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t252 container normal upper H 2 normal upper O\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t253 text .\n\t\t\t\t\t\t254 text This decomposition into a static nonlinear demand–headloss relation and a dynamic linear time-invariant (LTI) model yields a tractable representation suitable for controller design and deployment on standard industrial hardware, without requiring a hydraulic solver.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t255 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t256 text Compared with previous approaches, this work differs in three ways: (1) the dynamic model used for controller synthesis is identified from real measurements rather than a simulator; (2) the resulting low-order model is explicitly tailored for PLC/SCADA implementation; and (3) validation uses real consumption scenarios rather than artificially generated demand patterns. In this sense, the proposed methodology complements field studies such as Fontana et al. (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t257 link 2018b, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c19-4\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t258 text ): while their focus is field implementation, our contribution is a systematic workflow that enables practitioners to identify an accurate reduced-order model, design a reference signal tracking (RST) controller with explicit performance criteria, and test it off-line with real data prior to deployment.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t259 container sec-1-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t260 heading Contributions and Gap Addressed, Value: 3\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t261 text Contributions and Gap Addressed\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t262 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t263 text This paper bridges advanced RTC concepts and practical deployment by providing a transparent, data-driven, repeatable methodology for modeling and controlling pressure at a remote critical node in a real WDN sector. The main contributions are:\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t264 content list\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t265 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t266 text 1. Data-driven dynamic modeling of a PRV–network sector.  Using field measurements, we identify a low-order linear dynamic model with transport delay linking PRV pilot actuation to remote-node pressure. Static nonlinear demand–headloss behavior is separated from the dynamic response, yielding a controller-oriented representation.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t267 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t268 text 2. Discrete-time RST controller design with Smith predictor.  Based on the identified model, we design a digital RST controller with explicit delay compensation, enabling systematic specification of closed-loop dynamics, disturbance rejection, and tracking performance.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t269 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t270 text 3. Validation with real consumption data.  The controller is tested in silico using an independent 24-h dataset of real consumption patterns, demonstrating accurate pressure regulation and robustness to demand variability.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t271 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t272 text 4. Implementation readiness.  The workflow is designed for implementation on standard PLC/SCADA systems using existing telemetry and PRV hardware, without specialized devices or hydraulic solvers.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t273 text A “Limitations and Future Work” subsection clarifies that the controller is validated only in silico and that field deployment is the natural next step.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t274 text The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. The subsequent section details the modeling process for remote-node pressure, including the hydraulic sector description, instrumentation, model identification, disturbance modeling, and validation. The next section presents the controller design and the closed-loop evaluation under real demand data. Finally, the paper summarizes the main conclusions and outlines future work.\n\t\t\t\t\t275 container sec-2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t276 heading Modeling of Remote-Node Pressure, Value: 2\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t277 text Modeling of Remote-Node Pressure\n\t\t\t\t\t\t278 text Designing a remote-node pressure regulation system requires a model that relates remote-node pressure dynamics to PRV actuation and variations in consumer demand. For regulation around a nominal operating point, however, it is standard practice to approximate dominant dynamics with a linear time-invariant (LTI) model (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t377 link Åström and Murray 2021, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c1-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t378 text ). In this work, nonlinearities and slow parameter variations not captured by the LTI model are treated as disturbances. The choice of an RST controller with integral action is deliberate: integral action removes steady-state errors caused by model mismatch and low-frequency disturbances (\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t379 link Villanova and Visioli 2012, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…, ID: core-c45-1\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t380 text ), ensuring convergence of remote-node pressure to its reference.\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t381 container\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t382 text The workflow is illustrated in Fig. \n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t383 link Description: 3, Value: ascelibrary.org/doi…\n\t\t\t\t\t\t\t\t384 text . The resulting deviation responses overlap closely, indicating consistent PRV–network dynamics over the tested range. Dynamic Pressure Regulation in Water Distribution 
Systems through Remote Critical Node Control: 
A Case Study
A. Arango-Martinez1; E. Rosero-Garc�a2; and E. Franco-Mejia, Ph.D.3
Abstract: This paper presents a field-oriented methodology for modeling and controlling pressure regulation, demonstrated on a real water 
distribution network (WDN) sector. The aim is to provide a practical and reproducible pathway from field measurements to deployable 
real-time pressure control, addressing the gap between simulator-based numerical studies and full-scale field implementations. The study 
covers data-driven dynamic modeling, controller design, and pre-deployment performance assessment using real operational data. A low-order 
linear dynamic model with transport delay is identified directly from field measurements, combining 20 days of pressure and inflow data with 
step-response tests performed on an existing pressure-reducing valve (PRV). The model is validated against an independent 24-h dataset of real 
consumption, achieving a maximum deviation of approximately �1 m H2O at the remote critical node, which is adequate for control-oriented 
modeling and controller synthesis. Based on the identified model, a digital reference signal tracking (RST) controller with a Smith predictor is 
designed using explicit performance specifications for settling time, overshoot, and bandwidth. The controller is evaluated in silico using real 
consumption data not used during identification, providing an unbiased assessment of closed-loop performance under representative demand 
variability. Results show that the proposed strategy maintains pressure at the remote critical node within acceptable bounds while effectively 
rejecting demand-induced disturbances. Although closed-loop field implementation is beyond the scope of this study, the framework is explicitly 
conceived as a preparatory step for real-time deployment. The main conclusion is that a transparent, data-driven methodology grounded in field 
measurements can reduce modeling and tuning effort while yielding controller designs compatible with standard programmable logic controller 
(PLC) and supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) platforms, enabling utilities to progress systematically toward practical real-time 
pressure control. DOI: 10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321. � 2026 American Society of Civil Engineers.
Practical Applications: This study offers practical guidance for water utilities seeking to improve pressure management while reducing water 
losses and infrastructure wear. Using real operational data, the proposed approach shows how effective pressure regulation strategies can be 
derived directly from field measurements and implemented with standard PLC and SCADA systems already used by most utilities. As a result, 
the methodology is accessible without requiring advanced hydraulic-modeling expertise or specialized simulation tools. Pressure management 
is particularly important because daily demand variations

### stdout

````text
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.

```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final response citations

Place `:codex-file-citation{...}` inline in prose, not in a trailing list. Use `purpose="source"` for Q&amp;A/no-op and `purpose="output"` for create/edit.

- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Create/edit: cite each final PDF exactly once with a plain output citation. Summarize representative changes; do not cite every page or add a separate filename, path, or Markdown link. Example: `Created :codex-file-citation{path="/abs/path/report.pdf" purpose="output"}, with the completed analysis and appendix.`
- Q&amp;A/no-op: do not edit or re-export. Inspect the complete relevant pages, preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes, and cite each source PDF once with a plain source citation.

PDF citations currently support only plain file citations. Do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators. Never cite rendered PNGs, scratch files, builders, or QA intermediates unless asked.

### Final response suggested followups

- After successfully creating, editing, or analyzing an artifact, finish your final response with 3 useful next actions tailored to the artifact and the user's goal. Completing the requested work is not a reason to skip these actions: they help the user continue from the result. Do not perform the actions unless the user chooses one.
- Each action must be a distinct, concrete request the user could send next. Do not repeat completed work or invent filler. Omit the list if the user declines suggestions, the requested work fails, or no genuinely useful next action remains.
- Present each action as an unescaped Markdown list item using this exact syntax: `- :codex-followup[Short action]{prompt="Complete request for that action"}`.

--- PDF PAGE 1 ---
Milestone 1
Proposed Datasets for Pressure Prediction
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
NJIT ID: 31748996
|
UCID: aa3978
Area of interest. My research interests include pressure management in drinking-water distribution systems, particularly pressure
behavior at remote critical nodes. I have previously worked on this problem using field measurements and model-based control methods,
as described in a published case study. For this project, I would like to approach a related problem from a machine-learning perspective:
whether supervised learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to predict future pressure at a monitored location.
Dataset selection rationale. These two datasets are being considered because they align with the research interests of the project
and are supported by official data sources, network models, and published benchmark studies. Both contain simulated SCADA data,
and the number of records is substantially larger than the number of features. The amount of work currently available on Kaggle is
limited, Since the amount of work currently available on Kaggle is limited, the Milestone 2 review would be supplemented with literature
associated with these benchmarks. Pressure forecasting represents a different objective from the original challenges (leak detection and
cyberattack detection, respectively) although the specific contribution of the project would still need to be defined through that review.
Candidates at a glance
BattLeDIM: L-Town
BATADAL: C-Town

Official site + model + event records + benchmark
literature
Candidate 1
BattLeDIM: L-Town
Dataset
BattLeDIM provides simulated SCADA data for L-Town, a hypothetical network based on a real network in Cyprus.
Each of 2018 and 2019 contains 105,120 five-minute observations. Its 119 features comprise 33 pressures, three
flows, one tank level, and 82 AMR demand measurements. The official archive includes network models and leakage
information. The inventory on page 2 distinguishes measurements from event ground truth.
My proposed
task
I would predict a monitored node’s recorded pressure head, in meters, 15 minutes ahead using pressure, flow, tank
level, demand, and time information available at the forecast time. This is regression; pressure readings represent
five-minute averages. I would choose the node after reviewing topology and pressure behavior, without assuming it
is a critical node.
Why I chose it
Its distributed measurements and short sampling interval fit my interest in pressure management. The official
project site links a benchmark paper, and related studies. I would explore pressure forecasting, with leakage periods
as a possible later error analysis. I would use the official event files: the inspected Kaggle copy has a different 2019
leakage file.
Sources
Kaggle copy | Official Zenodo dataset | Benchmark site | Benchmark paper
Candidate 2
BATADAL: C-Town
Dataset
BATADAL contains hourly simulated C-Town SCADA data: seven tank levels, 12 flows, 12 discrete actuator states,
and 12 pressures, totaling 43 process variables. Its files contain 8,761 normal-operation, 4,177 mixed development,
and 2,089 test records. The official second set is partially labeled; the original test has no attack-label column.
Official attack-event lists are provided separately.
My proposed
task
I would predict recorded pressure head, in meters, at a selected monitored location one hour ahead from current
and past levels, actuator states, flows, and pressures. This is regression. Attack labels and future actuator states
would not be predictors. A later attack-period analysis would assess recorded signals, which may be falsified during
attacks, rather than assume they show true physical pressure.
Why I chose it
Pump and valve states make this a useful candidate for studying how operation relates to future pressure at an
hourly scale. The official site provides datasets, event lists, the C-Town model, rules, and a benchmark paper. I
would use those sources to check the Kaggle copy: its second set changes 3,958 unknown labels, and its test adds
labels absent from the original release.
Sources
Kaggle copy | Official datasets | Benchmark materials | Benchmark paper

I have previously worked on this problem using field measurements and model-based control methods, as described in a \\\\href{https://ascelibrary.org/doi/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321}{\\\\textit{published case study}}. For this project, I would like to approach a related problem from a machine-learning perspective: whether supervised learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to predict future pressure at a monitored location.\\r\\n\\\\end{contextbox}\\r\\n\\r\\n\\\\vspace{0.8mm}\\r\\n\\\\begin{rationalebox}\\r\\n  \\\\footnotesize\\r\\n  \\\\labeltext{Dataset selection rationale.}\\r\\nThese two datasets are being considered because they align with the research interests of the project and are supported by official data sources, network models, and published benchmark studies. Both contain simulated SCADA data, and the number of records is substantially larger than the number of features. The amount of work currently available on Kaggle is limited, Since the amount of work currently available on Kaggle is limited, the Milestone 2 review would be supplemented with literature associated with these benchmarks. 47-Abstract: A key challenge in designing algorithms for leakage detection and isolation in drinking water distribution systems is the
48-performance evaluation and comparison between methodologies using benchmarks. For this purpose, the Battle of the Leakage Detection
49-and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM) competition was organized in 2020 with the aim to objectively compare the performance of methods for
50:the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) measurements of flow and
51:pressure sensors installed within a virtual water distribution system. Several teams from academia and the industry submitted their solutions
52-using various techniques including time series analysis, statistical methods, machine learning, mathematical programming, met-heuristics,
53-and engineering judgment, and were evaluated using realistic economic criteria. This paper summarizes the results of the competition and
54-conducts an analysis of the different leakage detection and isolation methods used by the teams. The competition results highlight the need for
55-further development of methods for leakage detection and isolation, and also the need to develop additional open benchmark problems for this
56-purpose. DOI: 10.1061/(ASCE)WR.1943-5452.0001601. © 2022 American Society of Civil Engineers.
57-Introduction
58-Drinking water distribution networks (DWDNs) are susceptible to
59-infrastructure failures, which may lead to water losses. The global
60:average nonrevenue water (NRW) is 30%, with an estimated annual
61:cost of USD 39 billion (Liemberger and Wyatt 2019). A significant
62-part of NRW is due to background leakages and pipe bursts, which
63-may occur anywhere within the distribution network. Background
64-leakages are typically difficult to detect because of their small
65-size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate because they are
66-of larger size and may appear on the surface. The early detection
--
68-duces the time required for addressing the event and therefore
69-reducing the risk of further infrastructure degradation, contamina-
70-tion events, and consumer complaints. Leakage diagnosis in water
71-distribution systems has attracted a great deal of attention from
72-both practitioners and researchers over the past years (Chan et al.
73:2018). The process of leakage diagnosis can be separated into
74-leakage detection, which focuses on identifying the existence of
75-a leak in the network; and leakage localization, which aims to
76-provide an approximate location of leakages given the available
77:measurements. A recent review paper (Chan et al. 2018) classifies
78-leakage detection methodologies into passive and active methods.
79-Passive methods (also referred to as equipment-based, hardware,
80-or external methods) require the deployment of specialized equip-
81-ment, such as acoustic sensors or ground-penetrating radars, at
82-areas that are suspected of leakage. Active methods (also referred
83-to as internal or software methods) are methods that are based on
84-the presence of permanently installed sensors that continuously
85-monitor the system for leakages. The latest developments in hy-
86-draulic sensor technology and online data acquisition systems
87-have enabled water companies to deploy a larger number of more
88:accurate pressure and flow devices with less cost. These data can
89-be used to monitor the system in real time and develop method-
90-ologies that use the data to detect and prelocalize leaks using ac-
91-tive methods. Prelocalization is the process of defining an area in
92-which the leak exists instead of pinpointing exactly its location.
93-This research area has witnessed a significant interest, as indicated
94:in recent review papers (Li et al. 2015; Chan et al. 2018; Zaman
95:et al. 2019).
96-The term model-based leakage diagnosis is used to describe
97-methodologies that utilize a model of the DWDN (also referred
98-to as a numerical model) and sensor measurements to estimate
99-the steady-state hydraulic conditions in the network (Vrachimis
100:et al. 2018b). The operating principle behind model-based leakage
101-detection, as suggested by Pudar and Liggett (1992), is to find

132-June 9, 2022; published online on September 29, 2022. Discussion period
133-open until February 28, 2023; separate discussions must be submitted for
134-individual papers. This paper is part of the Journal of Water Resources

--
151-leakage diagnosis, methodologies for optimal placement of pres-
152-sure sensors are used (Farley et al. 2010; Casillas et al. 2013;
153-Cuguero-Escofet et al. 2017). Finally, the consideration of meas-
154-urement and model uncertainties is important when using these
155-methods to determine if the network is operating in a normal state
156:(Vrachimis et al. 2019) and should be taken into account before
157-making a decision about the occurrence of a leakage in the net-
158:work (Vrachimis et al. 2018a).
159-Leakage localization methods are typically model based be-
160-cause of the limited information provided by the small number of
161-sensors; one of the first representative examples is the work in Wu
162-et al. (2009), where the authors developed a model-based ap-
163-proach for leak localization that was applied to a large real system.
164-Another interesting model-based approach applied on real systems
165:is found in Sophocleous et al. (2019), where the authors formu-
166-lated an optimization problem to perform leakage diagnosis and
167-deal with the dimentionality of the problem using search space
168-reduction to reduce decision variables. Some approaches relate
169-the acquired measurements with the simulated output from many
170-simulated leakage scenarios on different locations of the network
171-(Farley et al. 2010; Goulet et al. 2013); the geographical mapping
172-of each model component can then be used to indicate the prob-
173-ability that a zone contains a leakage (Perez et al. 2014). Research-
174:ers have also used pressure residual analysis by creating a system
175:pressure sensitivity matrix to identify the location of leaks based
176-on the assumption of a single leakage occurring in the system
177-(Pérez et al. 2011; Cuguero-Escofet et al. 2017). A more recent
178-approach considers modeling uncertainties to create a set-bounded
179-model of the system and then incorporates sensor measurements
180-in an optimization-based framework to detect and prelocalize leak-
--
193-et al. (2002), where the authors introduced artificial neural net-
194-works (ANNs) for burst detection and continued to extend their
195-work in the following years (Mounce et al. 2010). Another ap-
196-proach is found in Eliades and Polycarpou (2012), where the au-
197-thors proposed an algorithm that analyzes the discrete inflow
198:signal of a district metered area (DMA) by using an adaptive
199-approximation methodology for updating the coefficients of a
200-Fourier series and detects leakages by utilizing the cumulative
201-sum (CUSUM) algorithm. Soldevila et al. (2016) used a mixed
202-model-based and data-driven approach to improve performance.
203-The study in Wu and Liu (2017) provides a review of data-driven
--
205-proaches are promising for use in real-life burst detection; how-
206-ever, reducing false alarms is still an important issue. Moreover, a
207-comprehensive performance evaluation procedure, especially
208-under different network configurations, might be necessary.
209-Leakage diagnosis methods are commonly evaluated on pri-
210:vate commercial data sets (Chan et al. 2018), and as a result it
211-is not possible to objectively compare different methods in their
212-ability to detect and isolate leaks. Moreover, data from real sys-
213-tems may not be readily available, while many aspects of the
214-system operation are unknown. For example, information about
215-the exact location, magnitude, and time profile of leakages is typ-
--
218-on real systems and having all the available information about the
219-system is the development of a realistic simulation benchmark
220-built on the expertise of practitioners, of which the operation re-
221-sembles that of a real system. Recently, a benchmark leakage de-
222-tection data set named LeakDB has been developed (Vrachimis
223:et al. 2018c); it was created using the Water Network Tool for
224-Resilience (WNTR) tool (Klise et al. 2017). The data set comprises
225:data generated from benchmark networks and uses pressure-
226-driven demands and realistic leakage modeling (van Zyl et al.
227-2017). In this work, a realistic open benchmark for leakage detec-
228-tion and localization is developed and used in a “battle” (Taormina
229:et al. 2018) to allow different teams to evaluate their methods in a
230-unified way.
231-The Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods
232-(BattLeDIM) was initially organized in 2020 as part of the Com-
233-puting and Control for the Water Industry and Water Distribution
234-Systems Analysis (CCWI/WDSA 2020) conference (which was
235-postponed due to the COVID-19 pandemic). The competition
236-aimed to objectively compare the performance of methods for the
237-detection and localization of leakage events, relying on supervisory
238:control and data acquisition (SCADA) measurements of flow and
239:pressure sensors generated using a realistic virtual city, which was
240:based on a real water distribution network in Cyprus. The overall
241-objective was to detect as many leakages as possible, as fast as pos-
242-sible, and as close to the source as possible, while avoiding false
243-alarms. Participants could use different types of tools and methods,
244-including (but not limited to) engineering judgment, machine learn-
245-ing, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault
--
247-industry around the world submitted their solutions to the compe-
248-tition, and the results were presented on an online workshop organ-
249-ized on September 3, 2020.
250-The main contributions of this work are (1) to introduce a new
251-benchmark network named L-Town, developed for the purposes of
252:the competition, along with a benchmark SCADA data set; (2) to
253-provide an overview of the different leakage and isolation method-
254-ologies presented at the BattLeDIM competition; and (3) to analyze
255-their results with respect to different objectives by proposing a
256-comprehensive evaluation procedure.
257-L-Town Benchmark Network
258-In this section, we introduce a new benchmark water distribution
259-network, which we refer to as L-Town. This is a city-scale model
260:inspired by a coastal city in Cyprus that can be used for research
261-purposes. The network was suitably modified and redesigned for
262-security purposes. L-Town is part of the KIOS Virtual City Testbed,
263:an open software platform for simulating the SCADA operation of
264-different critical infrastructures, including water, power, telecom-
265-munications, and transportation systems.
266-© ASCE
267-04022068-2
268-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.

274-Topology and Structure
275-The L-Town model, depicted in Fig. 1, is represented using the
276-EPANET version 2.2 (Rossman et al. 2020) input file format. It
277:has 782 junctions and 905 pipe segments of approximately
278-50 m length each and delivers drinking water to around 10,000 con-
279-sumers and industries. It comprises a network of steel pipes with a
280-total length of 42.6 km and roughness coefficients (C values) be-
281-tween 120 and 140. The L-Town network has a loop ratio of 25%, a
282-measure of complexity when solving the hydraulics of the network;
283-it indicates that 25% of the pipes have to be removed to eliminate
284:all loops from the network (Vrachimis et al. 2019). The node el-
285-evations range between 1.5 and 75 m above the sea level.
286-The water distribution network of L-Town receives water from
287:two reservoirs, and it was designed to provide pressure head of at
288:least 20 m to all of its consumers. The normal operating pressure in
289:the network ranges between 20 and 30 m. A pressure reduction
290-valve (PRV) was installed at the lower part of the town (Area B)
291-to help reduce background leakages. The network has different
292:pressure areas, and therefore exhibits different sensitivity to leak-
293-ages. PRVs were also installed downstream of the two main reser-
294:voirs to help regulate the pressure. A pump and a water tank were
295-installed in the higher part of the town (Area C) to provide suffi-
296:cient pressure to the consumers of that area. The tank has a diameter
297-of 16 m with a cylindrical shape. The pump was programmed so the
298-tank refills during the night and empties to Area C during the day.
299-The design decision to include pipes of 50 m length was based
300-on the following considerations: First, it is common for a real net-
301-work to have consumer demand locations at a 50-m interval; thus,
--
338-where db
339-i = base demand of node i; n = number of consumer types;
340-αj
341-i = percentage of the jth consumer type at the ith node; βj

343:average amount of water consumed in m3=h for each m2 of a

--
352---- PDF PAGE 6 ---
353-building; and γi = total building area corresponding to node i. In
354-this benchmark, three types of consumers (n ¼ 3) were considered:
355-residential, commercial, and industrial.
356-Each node has a unique demand pattern for each consumer
357:type based on the statistical characteristics of real metered data
358-from the area. Specifically, a Fourier series model was used to
359:approximate the demands (Vrachimis et al. 2018c), capturing sea-
360-sonality (weekly, yearly) as well as the uncertainties on demand
361-patterns (Fig. 2). The overall water consumption is the linear com-
362-bination of the base demands with the corresponding patterns.
363-The demand peaking factor, which is the ratio of the maximum
364:daily demand (MDD) to the average daily demand (ADD) in a
365-water system, was also considered in the design of demand pat-
366-terns. The ratio, based on observations from real systems, typically
367-ranges from 1.2 for very large water systems to 3.0 or even higher
368-for specific small systems. The demand peaking factor in L-Town
369:ranges between 1.5 and 2.0, given it is an average size system.
370-BattLeDIM Challenge Scenario
371-As part of the Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation
372-Methods, all participating teams were given the following artificial
373-scenario to establish the challenge:
374-In previous years, the utility of L-Town was experiencing a
375-large number of pipe breaks and water losses, affecting its
376:service quality. During 2018, a number of leakage events oc-
377-curred, which were detected and fixed by the water utility.
378-However, it is believed that a number of smaller leakages oc-
379-curred but not revealed. It is also assumed that some leakages
380-occurred abruptly, whereas others developed gradually, as
381-incipient events, from background leaks into pipe bursts.
--
384-bution network, in which base demands were assigned to nodes,
385-following historical billing data of proximity consumers. Moreover,
386-two nominal demand patterns were identified for residential and
387-commercial consumer types (with some discrepancies). The utility
388-believes that there might be some inaccuracies in the model,
389:e.g., with respect to the pipe roughness and pipe diameters.
390-In addition, the utility was not able to confirm the status of all the
391-valves in the network (i.e., whether they are open or closed).
392-The L-Town water utility is searching for a solution to help
393:them analyze the SCADA dataset, and detect leakage events as
394-fast as possible. In addition, it is crucial for the utility to have
395-an indication where approximately the leakage occurs, so that
396-the field workers can inspect those potential leaks using their
397-equipment.
398-The L-Town utility has created an open call for teams to dem-
399-onstrate their ability in detecting and localizing leakage events.
400:The teams are given a historical SCADA dataset along with infor-
401-mation related with the leakages detected and fixed by the utility
402:throughout 2018, to use for training purposes and for calibrating
403-their models. It is possible that more leakage events occurred
404:during 2018, however the utility was not able to detect and local-
405-ize them.
406:Throughout 2019, the utility conducted periodic surveys using
407-additional sensing equipment, pipe inspections and other methods,
408-and was able to detect and isolate all the leakage events that oc-
409-curred within that period. The most critical of these events were
410-repaired, however it was not possible to repair some of these leak-
411-ages due to financial reasons.
412-The overall goal of this competition, is to identify methods
413-which are able to detect and localize the leakage events that oc-
414:curred in L-Town in 2019, as fast as possible (with respect to time)
415-and as accurately as possible (with respect to their location), in or-
416-der to minimize their overall financial costs, both in water losses, as
417-well as due to the hours spent in isolating the leakage by the utility
418-staff. The L-Town utility will compare the different solutions and
419-select the best one based on that objective.
420-Scenario Generation and Available Data
421:To replicate the conditions of a real system, a SCADA data set was
422-synthetically generated using simulation to correspond to sensor
423-measurements from 2 years of system “operation.” For the gener-
424:ation of this SCADA data set, a virtual test-bed engine was de-
425-signed in Python, released under the European Union Public
426-License (EUPL) Open Source license. This test bed uses the
427-L-Town EPANET benchmark and incorporates a number of as-
428-sumptions with respect to the hydraulic solving, the leakage
429-500
--
496-of sensors.
497-Simulation and Data Set Generation Engine
498-The data set generation engine takes as input a structured file data-
499-set_configuration.yalm, which includes the start and end time of the
500-simulation, the leakages (including the start and end time, the leak
501:diameter, the type of the leakage, and its peak time), and the loca-
502:tions of the sensors [flow, pressure, automated metered readings
503:(AMRs), and level sensors].
504-The hydraulic simulations were executed using WNTR, a
505:Python package that supports pressure-driven demand simulations
506-and leakage modeling (Klise et al. 2017). Specifically, for the
507:pressure-driven demands, we computed a new demand for the
508-ith node DiðkÞ using the function fPDD, such that DiðkÞ ¼
509:fPDDðpiðkÞ; diðkÞÞ, where piðkÞ is the pressure and diðkÞ is the re-
510:quested demand at node i: If the computed pressure is piðkÞ < P0
511:then the demand is zero, i.e., DiðkÞ ¼ 0. If the pressure is piðkÞ >
512-Pf, then the demand equals the requested demand, i.e., DiðkÞ ¼
513:diðkÞ. Finally, in the case where the pressure is P0 ≤piðkÞ ≤Pf,
514-then the demand is calculated as DiðkÞ ¼ diðkÞððpiðkÞ −P0Þ=
515:ðPf −P0ÞÞδ. In BattLeDIM, we consider the following parameters:
516-P0 ¼ 7, Pf ¼ 25, and δ ¼ 0.5. The values for Pf and δ are the
517:default values used in WNTR, while the minimum pressure value
518-P0 ¼ 7 was increased from 3.5 to 7 m because this minimum
519-value was never observed in the L-Town network during the con-
520-sidered scenarios.
521:Using the pressure-dependent demand simulation, the node de-
522-mand DiðkÞ starts to decrease compared to the requested demand
523:diðkÞ when the pressure is below Pf and goes to zero when pressure
524-is below P0.
525-Nominal and Real Models
526-In practice, it is difficult to have an accurate model of the real
527-system. For this reason, a nominal EPANET L-Town model
528-was provided to the BattLeDIM participants; however, a real
529-model (which was unknown to the competitors) was used to gen-
530:erate the SCADA data set. In general, the nominal model approx-
531-imates the real, with some uncertainties. The nominal model was
532:generated by randomizing parameters of the real L-Town network
533-using the EPANET-MATLAB Toolkit (Eliades et al. 2016), as
534-follows:
535-•
536-Base demands: Base demands for each consumer type at each
537-node are randomized uniformly between 10% compared to the
--
541-are available; however, industrial patterns are not available. The
542-patterns used in the real model are unique for each node and may
543-differ significantly from the nominal patterns, while they also
544-include a significant noise component.
545-•
546:Pipe parameter uncertainty: All pipe parameters (roughness,
547:length, and diameter) are randomized uniformly between
548-10% of their real value. This randomization aims to represent
549-the uncertainty of hydraulic resistance, which is a function of all
550:the aforementioned pipe parameters. In reality, parameter uncer-
551-tainties may have different magnitudes. Typically, the most
552:uncertain parameter is pipe roughness, while pipe length and
553:diameter are less uncertain.
554-•
555-Topological uncertainty: Two pipes (p37 and p251) were ran-
556-domly selected to be closed in the real network, whereas in
557-the nominal model they appeared to be open. The term topologi-
558-cal uncertainty is used here to describe the variability of the
--
562-during operations, such as repairs, that have taken place in
563-the network.
564-Sensors and Telemetry
565-We assume that there is one tank water level sensor, a total of three
566-flow sensors, one at the pump and one at each of the DMA entran-
567:ces, and 33 pressure sensors, all transmitting their measurements
568:every 5 min to the utility’s SCADA system. There are no time de-
569:lays in the data transmission and no lost packages. Pressure sensors
570:give an average value of the last 5 min, which mitigates the uncer-
571:tainty due to pressure transients in the system. In addition, 82
572:AMRs were installed in Area C for delivering water consumption
573:data directly to the SCADA system. Each AMR gives the aggre-
574:gated consumption of many users in the AMR area.
575:The locations of the pressure sensors are depicted in Fig. 3, and
576:the AMRs in Fig. 4. Sensor readings do not have errors nor time
577-delays. The simulated sensor readings are rounded to two decimal
578-points; in practice this reduces the amount of data sent over the
579-telecommunications network.
580-Leakage Modeling
581-We assume that the only faults affecting the system during the
582-2-year operation are background leakages and pipe bursts. Any
583-preexisting leakages in the network are assumed to be small rel-
584-ative to individual node demands and have been incorporated into
585:the pressure-dependent demands of the network. To model the
586-leakage outflow in the ith node, we assume the following general
587-model (Lambert 2001; Greyvenstein and van Zyl 2007; Cassa
588-et al. 2010):
589-liðkÞ ¼ LðkÞ½piðkÞζ
590-ð2Þ
--
601-related to the characteristics of the leak is assumed to be ζ ¼ 0.5.
602-A key aspect is the leakage magnitude and the time profile of the
603-leakages. There are three types of leaks in the system, categorized
604-depending on their magnitude:
605-1. Background leaks: Small leaks with size of 0%–5% of the
606:average inflow.
607-2. Medium pipe bursts: Pipe breaks with flow size of 5%–10% of
608:the average inflow.
609-3. Large pipe bursts: Pipe breaks with flow size above 10% of the
610:average inflow.
611:In general, the average system inflow for the benchmark is around
612-180 m3=h. The concept of background leaks is based on the
613-categorization presented in Lambert (1994); these are leakages that
614-may exist in the system undetected for a long period of time. In the
615-proposed benchmark, the smallest background leak was constrained
616:at 2.5% of the average inflow to enable their detection. The distinc-
617-tion between medium and large pipe bursts is made assuming the
618-latter are made visible and fixed more quickly by the water utility.
619-Moreover, the leak hole area AðkÞ can be time-varying. In the
620-case of abrupt leakage, the hole area is zero before the leakage start
621-time T0, and becomes A after that time
--
662-data set and continue until the end, or they can start at any given
663-time; (2) there are no large pipe bursts that started before the sim-
664-ulation time; and (3) background leaks can evolve into bursts
665-(incipient leaks), e.g., a background leak that may have started
666-as a small crack on a pipe may evolve into a large burst due to
667:the stress applied on the pipe by pressure transients.
668-Leakage Reporting
669-In practice, large leakages are easier to identify and fix because they
670-will be reported at some point by consumers or the utility staff. For
671-the data set leakages, we assume that large pipe bursts are detected
672-and fixed by the water utility if they reach a flow magnitude larger
--
676-within 1 week after Td. After the leak is fixed, the area of the leak
677-hole becomes zero, i.e., AðkÞ ¼ 0; t > Tr. Specifically, large and
678-some medium-size leakages (above 15 m3=h) are fixed by the
679-water utility after a reasonable time selected at random, with
680-maximum delay of 2 months.

693-constraints and assumptions:
694-•
695-Based on the size of the network, statistically around 15 leakage
696-(background and burst) events should appear each year in the
697-network, with a maximum of 20 events. Eventually, we assume
698:14 events in the year 2018 and 2019 events in the year 2019. Four
699:background leaks in the year 2018 continued in the year 2019.
700-Only large pipe bursts are detected and fixed by the water utility.
701-•
702-We assume that at most two pipe bursts can coexist in the net-
703-work during the examined periods. This is to enforce a wider
704-spreading of the leaks during the year.
--
711-•
712-We assume that in case leakages exist with overlapping
713-detecting radii, there is a minimum 2-week difference between
714-their start times. This is to ensure separability of the alerts during
715-the evaluation phase.
716:The final leakage locations for year 2018 and 2019 are found in
717-the Figs. 5 and 6, respectively. The time profile of the leakages in
718:2019 is depicted in Fig. 7.
719-BattLeDIM Data Sets
720-The BattLeDIM data sets are composed of the following files,
721-which are openly accessible via the Zenodo platform under the
722-FAIR principles:
723-•
724-Configuration files: The data set configuration file indicates
725:the simulation period as well as the characteristics of the 33 si-
726-mulated leakages as part of BattLeDIM. It also specifies the sen-
727:sors to be included in the SCADA data sets. The file format
728-is YAML.
729-•
730:SCADA data sets: These correspond to the SCADA measure-
731:ments during the 2-year period between January 1, 2018, at
732:00:00 and December 31, 2019, at 23:55, at 5-min time steps.
733:The SCADA data sets are comprised of the water tank level,
734:the flow sensors, the AMR measurements, and the pressure sen-
735-sors. The file format is CSV.
736-•
737-Leakages: Table of times with respect to the leakage events of
738-BattLeDIM, indicating their outflows in m3=h. The file format
739-is CSV.
740-•
741-Fixed leakages reports: This includes the repair times of pipe
742:bursts that were fixed in 2018 by the water utlity. The file format
743-is TXT.
744-•
745-Network models: Two network models are provided: The real
746-model is the one used to generate the 2-year data sets, along with
747:all the demand patterns. It contains the real network parameters
748-and consumer demands. It does not contain any leakages. The
749-real network should be considered as “unknown.” The nominal
750-model should be used as the “known” model. This network is
751:provided with nominal parameters for all the system elements.
752:The nominal base demands for each node are based on average
753:historical metered consumption. Weekly demand profiles for
754-three consumer types (residential, commercial, and industrial)
755-are also provided; however, they do not capture the yearly sea-
756:sonality. Furthermore, the EPANET model parameters may be
757:different from the actual network parameters (e.g., diameters,
758:Fig. 5. Location of leakages in 2018 data set.

--
781-algorithms is for them to be applicable in real systems and be able
782-to deal with the problems arising from the scarcity and reliability
783-of the data collected from the field. The aim of the proposed
784-benchmark is to offer a realistic simulation scenario built on the
785-expertise of practitioners that closely resembles real conditions. It
786:has the advantage that all the parameters and aspects of the system
787-operation are known, and thus it can be used to compare and
788-evaluate different methodologies. However, it has limitations and
789-differences from real systems, which are stated in this section to
790-advise caution to researchers and practitioners when using the
791-benchmark.
792-The realistic demands included in this benchmark were gener-
793-ated by analyzing demands from real networks into their compo-
794-nents and reproducing them by randomizing the components as
795:described in Vrachimis et al. (2018c). Real network demands
796-may vary compared to the proposed approximations. Moreover,
797:pressure-driven analysis is used to make the demands more realis-
798-tic; however, more research may be needed in selecting appropriate
799:values for the pressure-driven analysis parameters.
800-A realistic leakage modeling approach was followed in this
801:work by modeling pressure-dependent leakages on pipes, while
802-the leakage function is constructed such as to exhibit time vari-
803-ability with respect to the orifice size. However, the function de-
804-scribing leakage flow may vary in practice because data collection
805-about the size of leaks found in the field is a challenging task.
806-More realistic leakage functions than the one used in Eq. (2) have
--
819-time processing of data may be impeded by measurements arriving
820-at later time steps or never arriving at all.
821-This benchmark does not take into account events that may hap-
822-pen during and after repair works. Typically, repairs require the iso-
823-lation of network sections by closing valves, an action that may
824:cause pressure increase in the network. A typically observed phe-
825-nomenon is the increase of leakage flows in other parts of the net-
826-work during repairs or, in the worst cases, new pipe bursts. The risk
827-of causing new leakages during repairs was not taken into account
828-and should be considered when using this benchmark to test leak-
829-age diagnosis methodologies designed for application on real
--
839-Methods
840-In the following paragraphs, we provide a short overview of the
841-methodologies proposed by the competing teams.
842-The Cheng00 team (Cheng et al. 2020) resorted to a three-stage
843-approach involving simulation, ensemble multivariate change point
844:detection (EMCPD), and statistical analysis. Pressure and flow
845:residual time series were first obtained by comparing the SCADA
846-data sets with those of simulated normal operation, produced with
847-the provided benchmark model. The residuals were then analyzed
848-with EMCPD to obtain a rough estimate of the occurrence of leak
849-events in space and time. The final localization was performed after
850:interpolating nodal pressures around likely candidate positions
851-and by isolating the most likely sites with a two-sample one-sided
852-Student’s t-test.
853-The DandW team (Huang et al. 2020, 2022) proposed a
854-methodology that treats each area of the L-Town network in Fig. 1
855-separately. This method exploited the provided benchmark model
856-to estimate expected sensor readings during normal operations
857:and compute the residuals with respect to the provided SCADA
858-data. Sensitivity vectors were then computed for each pipe as
859:the Jacobian matrix of nodal pressures to pipe flows. The angle
860-method, which involves calculating the angle between the residual
861-vectors and the sensitivity vectors, was then used to isolate leaky
862-pipes. These are characterized by having the smallest angles.
863-The Leakbusters team (Daniel et al. 2020, 2022) tackled the
864:challenge with a high-resolution pressure-driven method for leak-
865-age identification and localization composed of two sequential
866-modules. In the first module, linear regression models were cali-
867:brated using data with no leaks to predict pairwise sensor pressure
868:readings. When fed with new SCADA data, the reconstruction error
869-between predicted and observed readings was tracked to identify
870-the start time of a potential leak and the location of its nearest sen-
871-sor. The second module used the start time and most affected sen-
872-sors reported by the first module to pinpoint leaky pipes relying on
873-an initial set of candidate pipes and the application of a simulation-
--
875-integer linear programming.
876-The CIACUA team (Saldarriaga et al. 2020) approached the
877-BattLeDIM problem by resorting to anomaly detection analysis
878-and a simulation-optimization framework involving EPANET and
879-genetic algorithms (GAs). Anomaly detection analysis was first
880:carried out by comparing SCADA data and the output of EPANET
881-models. If the error between any observed and predicted signals
882-passed a certain threshold, simulation-based optimization with GA
883-was used to find which location would best explain such discrep-
884-ancy, thus identifying the leaking pipe. Emitter equations were used
885-to simulate leaks in the EPANET model.
--
887-approach where statistical methods were used in combination with
888-hydraulic modeling. Their scheme comprised three stages. In the es-
889-timation stage, empirical model decomposition (EMD) and vector
890-autoregressive models were used to estimate expected flow and pres-
891-sure in normal conditions. The residuals between these expected val-
892:ues and observed SCADA data were further processed in the
893-identification stage to place leaks in time and infer their size. In
894-the final localization stage, leaking pipes were isolated by a double
895:comparison between observed and simulated (EPANET) pressure
896-data for the week with the suspected leak and the one preceding it.
897:The Under Pressure team (Steffelbauer et al. 2020, 2022) also
898-employed a hierarchical approach made of three stages. Similar to
899-the Tsinghua team, in the first stage demand calibration for the en-
900:tire network was inferred from AMR data on Zone C using EMD.
901-© ASCE
902-04022068-9

904- J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage., 2022, 148(12): 04022068 
905-Downloaded from ascelibrary.org by Technische Universiteit Delft on 01/09/23. Copyright ASCE. For personal use only; all rights reserved.
