909-They also performed a calibration of the roughness coefficient
910-using a weighted least-squares problem with bounded constraints.
911:The second stage of Under Pressure’s approach entailed the crea-
912-tion of a dual hydraulic model for leak detection. In this dual model,
913:the pressure drops due to a leak translate into additional outflows to
914:virtual reservoirs connected to the pressure measurement nodes.
915-These time series, and the derived residuals, have a much better
916-signal-to-noise ratio, which facilitates detection and localization.
917-This is done in the third stage, where leaks were first identified
918-in time with the help of change detection methods (CUSUM, like-
919-lihood-ratio) and GA. The leaking pipe was then isolated based
--
922-DandW team.
923-Fuzzy methods were at the core of the Zhiyun Shuiwu team
924-(Zhang et al. 2020). In the first stage, deep fuzzy mapping was used
925-to calibrate model demands from observations. Second, leaks were
926-identified in time based on anomalies between observed and mod-
927:eled pressure values and an analysis of the most affected nodes.
928-Localization was finally performed based on fuzzy similarity be-
929-tween real bursts characteristics and pipe network characteristics.
930-The IRI team (Romero et al. 2020; Romero-Ben et al. 2022)
931-devised a data-driven approach for Area A of L-Town due to the
932:high density of pressure sensors. On the other hand, a model-based
933-approach was used for both Areas B and C to respectively over-
934:come the lack of pressure sensors and exploit the availability of
935:AMRs. In the data-driven approach, graph-based interpolation was
936-first performed to estimate the state of the entire network from
937-available data of leaky and nonleaky scenarios. The selection of
938:candidate leak location was then performed by nodal pressure com-
939-parison between these estimated states. In the model-based ap-
940-proach, EPANET simulations were carried out after inferring
941-the demands for Areas B and C. The results of the simulations with
942-leaks added at different locations were compared against the
943:SCADA data to find the most likely placement for the leak.
944-The KU Hydrosystems team (Min et al. 2020) proposed a two-
945-stage method where leak identification in time and space was
946-tackled separately using a data-driven and a model-based approach.
947-After preprocessing the data and performing feature selection, the
948-detection of the leak in time was performed jointly by resorting to
--
960-may be attributed to leaks. Leaks were then detected by comparing
961-the difference between expected demands (derived from flow pro-
962-files of five preceding days based on cluster membership) and ob-
963-served flows. If the residuals indicate the presence of a leak, hot
964-spots were localized by solving a regularized inverse problem that
965:includes a pressure-driven model for the leak flow.
966-DHI China (Liu et al. 2020) proposed a method that relies on
967-genetic algorithms and machine learning (ML) techniques. GA was
968-used to calibrate the provided nominal model, whose demand pat-
969:terns were defined based on the analysis of the provided AMR data.
970-Leak detection in time was done with the use of both deep learning
971-methods (a long short-term memory neural network) and gradient-
972-boosted trees (LightGBM). GA-based simulation optimization
973-(with EPANET) was employed to localize the leaky pipe, similar
974-to what was done by other teams.
--
978-model calibration stage was performed to get a calibrated hydraulic
979-model using a time period where little or no leakages were assumed
980-to exist. Any preexisting leakages in the selected time period were
981-incorporated into the calibrated model, which was then used to
982-estimate the overall yearly leakage flows and to predict nodal
983:pressures under a leak-free scenario. Then, the pressure residuals
984:between observed and predicted pressure were processed by inte-
985-grating the seasonal and trend decomposition using Loess decom-
986-position method and the k-means clustering method to identify
987-different leak scenarios during the analysis period. Finally, by add-
988-ing not repaired but identified leaks to the calibrated hydraulic
989-model in the localization stage, a new and simple leakage scenario
--
992-stepwise method based on matching degrees between the actual
993-leakage feature and the simulated leakage features.
994-The Wu BSY team (Wu and He 2020) presented an integrated
995-data analysis with a hydraulics-based modeling approach consist-
996-ing of three main steps: (1) data preprocessing to prepare for analy-
997:sis, where flow and pressure time series are decomposed to get
998-rid of trend and seasonality using the Seasonal-Trend decomposi-
999-tion procedure; (2) data analysis for leakage event detection, where
1000-the decomposed time series are analyzed using statistical process
1001-control methods; and (3) model analysis, where simulation-based
1002-optimization in Bentley WaterGEMS, a hydraulic model calibration
1003:tool, is used to localize the leaky pipes using a pressure-driven ap-
1004-proach where the emitter coefficients and locations are the param-
1005-eters to be optimized.
1006-The CUBALYTICS team (Bhowmick and Seifert 2020) also de-
1007-vised an approach combining data-driven methods with hydraulic
1008-simulations. This method was based on the computation of an
--
1015-anomaly. Leaks were identified in time by analyzing contiguous
1016-rows in the AM having multiple anomalies. The list of nodes,
1017-i.e., the headers of all columns with nonzero entries, was checked
1018-to find valid node combinations identifying potential leaky pipes.
1019-The isolated pipe for each leak was selected after comparison with
1020:pressure-driven simulations.
1021-Decision trees were at the core of the methodology of the
1022-Artesia team (Adanza Dopazo 2020). The approach consists of
1023-three main steps. In the first step, data normalization and feature
1024-engineering was performed to extract minimum and maximum
1025:daily peaks as well as averages for different parts of the day for
1026:all pressure, water level, and flow sensors. Decision trees were then
1027:trained on this refined data set to predict the mean night pressure
1028:values expected for each pressure sensor. The mean pressure during
1029:the night was chosen as the target to predict because pressure
1030-during this time of the day is more steady and less affected by ran-
1031-domness. In the last stage, the differences between predicted and
1032:observed mean night pressure values in the test data set were used
1033-to identify leaks in time, while comparison of results across neigh-
1034:boring pressure sensors was used to improve localization.
1035-The DHI Singapore team (Tan et al. 2020) employed WNTR, a
1036-Python wrapper of EPANET, to generate extra data for training a
1037-© ASCE
1038-04022068-10
1039-J. Water Resour. Plann. Manage.
--
1042-
1043-
1044---- PDF PAGE 13 ---
1045-deep neural network (DNN) using Tensorflow. Before generating
1046-the leak events, the team calibrated the provided nominal model to
1047:find optimal values of pipe diameters and roughness coefficient, as
1048-well as determining optimal seasonality of residential and commer-
1049:cial demands. Calibration was performed using GA and the 2018
1050:pressure readings. The DNN development data set was generated
1051-from 400 simulations with random leaks at different locations, with
1052-different start times and durations. A five-hidden-layer DNN was
1053-trained on these data to isolate the leaky location having as inputs
1054:the readings from the 33 pressure sensors. After its validation, the
1055-DNN was tested on the competition data set.
1056-The UNIFE team (Marzola et al. 2020, 2022) adopted a prag-
1057-matic approach to detect and localize leakage events based on the
1058:analysis of the SCADA data and the use of the provided hydraulic
1059-model of the network. After inferring demand patterns for the entire
1060:network based on the provided AMR data, the hydraulic model was
1061:calibrated (roughness and diameters) to realistically represent the
1062-hydraulic behavior of the network. The observed inflows and water
1063-demands were then analyzed to identify leakage number, entity, and
1064-time of occurrence with engineering judgment. Each identified
1065-leakage was then spatially localized through an enumerative pro-
1066-cedure. This is done by (1) performing simulation after assigning
--
1092-model-based approaches relied on the use of nominal water net-
1093-work models provided (such as the EPANET L-Town model).
1094-To accommodate the differences between the measurements and
1095-the nominal model, calibration methods were used to design a more
1096-accurate representation by updating the demands and certain pipe
1097:parameters. The calibrated model can be used to create data sets
1098-describing the operation of the system under normal and faulty op-
1099-eration conditions, e.g., using the EPANET libraries. This can allow
1100:the comparison of the computed pressure residuals with the ob-
1101:served pressure sensor measurements.
1102-Another approach is to consider the mathematical model of the
1103:system, to create a pressure sensitivity matrix, through a linearization
1104-of the hydraulic equations. Using the matrix, residuals can be com-
1105-puted using model-based approaches that compare simulation-based
1106:estimations and SCADA measurements, as well as by using model-
1107-free approaches. The residuals, as well as other relevant time series,
1108-can be analyzed using change detection techniques (e.g., CUSUM,
1109-angle method), time series analysis and signal processing, empirical
1110-method decomposition, regression analysis, hypothesis testing, and
1111-other statistical approaches. More advanced statistical approaches,
--
1116-or on explicit mathematical formulations that can be solved using
1117-integer, dynamic, or mixed-integer programming. Where this is not
1118-possible due to the complexity of the optimization formulation,
1119-metaheuristics (such as genetic algorithms or particle swarm opti-
1120-mization) can be used. Finally, some approaches analyzed the
1121:AMR area in a different way, by creating a model of the water de-
1122-mands in the area, to exploit the additional information provided
1123:due to the significant penetration of the smart meters.
1124-Evaluation Procedure
1125-Participants were required to submit their results in the format
1126-specified in a template file, which includes the location and start
1127-time of each detected leakage event. The start time of a leakage is
1128-specified in the ISO 8601 time format YYYY-MM-DD hh:mm.
--
1140-given leakage start time is during the lifetime of the same leakage.
1141-The predefined pipe radius is defined by the capability of the close-
1142-range equipment used by the repair crew (e.g., acoustic sensors) to
1143-exactly pinpoint the location of the leakage in a single workday.
1144-The scoring methodology is described here in detail. Given a
1145:user-defined set of detections D and the set of leakages L (2019
1146-BattLeDIM data set), the total score S is calculated using the
1147-following rules:
1148-1. True detection (true positive): A given detection i ∈D is con-
1149-sidered a true detection of a leakage j ∈L if the detection time
1150-ti
--
1164-location.
1165-2. False detection [false positive (FP)]: False detections are the
1166-detections that do not satisfy the true detection condition.
1167-3. Missed detection (false negative): Missed detections are the set
1168-of leakages in L that have not been detected by any detection in
1169:D (includes four leakages starting in 2018 and 2019 leakages
1170:starting in 2019).

1621:The parameters of maximum detection radius xmax, cost of water
1622:per cubic meter in euro cw, and the maximum repair crew cost cr
1623-are given in Table 3. The cost of water was selected assuming a
1624:water utility that operates in Cyprus. The maximum repair crew
1625-cost was calculated assuming a three-person repair crew searching
1626-for the leakage location for a whole 8-h workday, with an hourly
1627-rate of approximately 20 euro. The maximum detection radius was
1628-selected assuming the repair crew is able to search using acoustic
1629-sensors a maximum pipe length of 1 km in a single workday. For
1630:this distance to be translated into a radius, an average of three pipe
1631-branches emerging around any given location is assumed. The
1632:maximum score in this problem, given the parameters of Table 3
1633-and the leakages existing in the data set, was achieved when all
1634-leakages were detected at their exact start time and location, while
1635-no false detections were given. The “perfect” score of the compe-
1636-tition was calculated using Eq. (9) to be €523,124.

1640:100 m3=h. The evaluation parameters were arbitrarily chosen as
1641-follows: cost of water cw ¼ €1=m3, maximum crew cost cr ¼

1835-not affect the number of true positives or false positives achieved by

1969-approaches were used by the teams and the robustness of each
1970-approach to different evaluation functions may vary. Some of the
1971-observations are discussed in the following:
1972-•
1973-Most top-scoring teams made use of a nominal model, of which
1974:the parameters were calibrated in some form using sensor data,
1975-to construct a water distribution model that describes the normal
1976:operation of the system (such as Tsinghua, Under Pressure,
1977-IRI, and UNIFE) by incorporating existing leakages into the
1978-calibrated node demands. This allows the computation of the
1979:expected flows and pressures at different locations in the net-
1980:work. Moreover, they also consider the AMR measurements
